  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, Dr. Smith, I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm just imagining things, but I know for a fact my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. It’s not in my head. I mean, my tools don't just move themselves. I’ve been an assembler down at the fabrication plant for thirty years, and when you work on a line that long, you learn exactly where you put your things. You have to, or the whole line backs up and the foreman starts yelling. We had this foreman back in the nineties, guys called him 'Bulldog' because of his neck... anyway, I know where I leave my wrenches. But at home, they keep getting moved around. 

And it's not just that. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. I usually like to head up to the north side of the reservoir—there's a quiet spot right past the old bridge where the water gets real deep, perfect for bass if you use a topwater lure early in the morning. I used to go out there in my old aluminum boat. But lately? Every weekend, something gets messed up on purpose so I can't go. 

After all these months of this going on, I can see it plain as day. He's been trying to take over my place and push me out of my own home. But my wife and everyone else just look at me like I'm making it all up.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Voices? No, doc, no. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. I'm not crazy. My hearing is fine—well, maybe a little ringing sometimes from the stamping presses at the plant, but that's just normal wear and tear from thirty years on the floor. 

And my eyes are perfectly fine. Although, I will say, it's getting a lot harder to see at work these days because of those new safety glasses they make us wear. They brought them in a few years back—said the old ones weren't up to the new OSHA codes or something. But these new ones, they scratch so easy, and they fog up the second you get a sweat going. You're trying to line up a bracket, and suddenly you're looking through a cloud. I told Dave, our safety rep, I said, 'Dave, how am I supposed to do precision work if I can't even see my own hands?' Dave just shrugs. He’s a decent guy, Dave is, but he’s got his hands tied by corporate up in Chicago. Those guys in suits, they’ve never worked a day on the line in their lives, but they want to tell us how to see. They changed the gloves on us last year, too, and those don't grip the wrenches right... 

Anyway, what was the question? Oh, right. No, no voices or ghosts or any of that movie stuff. Just the things actually happening right in front of me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Medical problems? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, my blood sugar is fine, thyroid is fine. I've always been pretty healthy. Honestly, the only time I ever really had to go to the doctor was back when my son was playing Little League. I think it was '98 or '99? He was playing shortstop, and I was helping coach the team. We had this one kid, Tommy, who threw wild. I mean, the boy had an arm like a cannon, but he couldn't aim to save his life. One practice, I wasn't looking—I was trying to untangle the equipment bag because somebody just threw all the catcher's gear in there without strapping it together—and BAM. Baseball right to the shoulder. Hurt like you wouldn't believe, but it wasn't my head. 

I did have to go to the clinic for that shoulder, though. They gave me this sling that chafed my neck so bad I couldn't wear it at the plant. You can't be on the assembly line with one arm strapped to your chest anyway, so I just worked through it. You know how it is, if you take time off, they just give your station to one of the new guys, and then you come back and your area is a mess. It takes weeks to get everything organized again. But yeah, no head injuries or diabetes or anything like that. Just that one time with the baseball.

It’s made things tense with my wife, but I’m still managing work and daily responsibilities.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 9 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about nine months, ever since my wife’s relative moved into our home.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / assembler_or_fabricator / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm Dr. Smith. It's nice to meet you; what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. Smith, I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm just imagining things, but I know for a fact my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. It’s not in my head. I mean, my tools don't just move themselves. I’ve been an assembler down at the fabrication plant for thirty years, and when you work on a line that long, you learn exactly where you put your things. You have to, or the whole line backs up and the foreman starts yelling. We had this foreman back in the nineties, guys called him 'Bulldog' because of his neck... anyway, I know where I leave my wrenches. But at home, they keep getting moved around. \n\nAnd it's not just that. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. I usually like to head up to the north side of the reservoir—there's a quiet spot right past the old bridge where the water gets real deep, perfect for bass if you use a topwater lure early in the morning. I used to go out there in my old aluminum boat. But lately? Every weekend, something gets messed up on purpose so I can't go. \n\nAfter all these months of this going on, I can see it plain as day. He's been trying to take over my place and push me out of my own home. But my wife and everyone else just look at me like I'm making it all up.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the tools being moved, have you been hearing voices or seeing things that other people don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, doc, no. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. I'm not crazy. My hearing is fine—well, maybe a little ringing sometimes from the stamping presses at the plant, but that's just normal wear and tear from thirty years on the floor. \n\nAnd my eyes are perfectly fine. Although, I will say, it's getting a lot harder to see at work these days because of those new safety glasses they make us wear. They brought them in a few years back—said the old ones weren't up to the new OSHA codes or something. But these new ones, they scratch so easy, and they fog up the second you get a sweat going. You're trying to line up a bracket, and suddenly you're looking through a cloud. I told Dave, our safety rep, I said, 'Dave, how am I supposed to do precision work if I can't even see my own hands?' Dave just shrugs. He’s a decent guy, Dave is, but he’s got his hands tied by corporate up in Chicago. Those guys in suits, they’ve never worked a day on the line in their lives, but they want to tell us how to see. They changed the gloves on us last year, too, and those don't grip the wrenches right... \n\nAnyway, what was the question? Oh, right. No, no voices or ghosts or any of that movie stuff. Just the things actually happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a doctor that you have any medical problems, like issues with your blood sugar, thyroid, or perhaps any history of head injuries?\n\nPatient: Medical problems? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, my blood sugar is fine, thyroid is fine. I've always been pretty healthy. Honestly, the only time I ever really had to go to the doctor was back when my son was playing Little League. I think it was '98 or '99? He was playing shortstop, and I was helping coach the team. We had this one kid, Tommy, who threw wild. I mean, the boy had an arm like a cannon, but he couldn't aim to save his life. One practice, I wasn't looking—I was trying to untangle the equipment bag because somebody just threw all the catcher's gear in there without strapping it together—and BAM. Baseball right to the shoulder. Hurt like you wouldn't believe, but it wasn't my head. \n\nI did have to go to the clinic for that shoulder, though. They gave me this sling that chafed my neck so bad I couldn't wear it at the plant. You can't be on the assembly line with one arm strapped to your chest anyway, so I just worked through it. You know how it is, if you take time off, they just give your station to one of the new guys, and then you come back and your area is a mess. It takes weeks to get everything organized again. But yeah, no head injuries or diabetes or anything like that. Just that one time with the baseball.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed any changes in your energy levels, personality, or your ability to focus and remember things over the past few years?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Energy? No, I got plenty of energy. I work a full shift on my feet, don't I? And my memory is sharp as a tack. I mean, I remember exactly where I leave my tools, like I told you. I can remember things from thirty years ago clear as a bell. Like the old breakroom at the plant before they remodeled it back in '06. 

We used to have this old vending machine in the corner that would always eat your quarters if you tried to buy the peanut butter crackers. You had to hip-check it right on the side—right next to the coin slot—to get your food to drop. Old man Miller, he used to get so mad at that machine. He'd be kicking it, swearing up a storm. He retired, gosh, must be fifteen years ago now. Moved down to Florida. Tampa, I think? Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf side. 

The fishing down there is supposed to be incredible, you know. Redfish, snook... I always wanted to get down there and try saltwater fishing. You need completely different gear for that, though. Heavy-duty reels. The salt just eats right through standard bearings if you don't rinse them off with fresh water the second you get off the boat. I use a little WD-40 on my freshwater reels at home, keeps them spinning nice and smooth, assuming nobody's been in the garage messing with my tackle box. 

But yeah, I don't know what you mean about my personality changing. I'm the exact same guy I've always been. I just want to be left alone in my own house, you know? I don't see how wanting my things left where I put them means I can't focus.

Somewhere on the Gulf side. \n\nThe fishing down there is supposed to be incredible, you know. Redfish, snook... I always wanted to get down there and try saltwater fishing. I use a little WD-40 on my freshwater reels at home, keeps them spinning nice and smooth, assuming nobody's been in the garage messing with my tackle box. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know what you mean about my personality changing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Seen him? Well, no, because he's sneaky about it. He waits until I'm at the plant. But I'm telling you, I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it. My wife, my neighbors... they look at me like I'm the one who's lost it. But I know my wife's relative is coming into this house to get at me. 

My tools don't move themselves, doc. And it's not just the tools anymore. My fishing plans get spoiled by accident every single weekend. Like last Saturday, I had the truck all packed. I was going to head up to Lake Shelbyville. You ever been up there? It's a man-made reservoir, Army Corps of Engineers built the dam back in the 70s. Great crappie fishing if you know where the submerged timber is. You gotta use these little tube jigs, chartreuse or maybe white with a pink jig head, and just drop it right down into the brush piles. If you're off by a foot, you're snagged. I must have lost a dozen jigs the last time I went. You spend half the morning just tying new knots. The Palomar knot is the best for that, though some guys swear by the improved clinch. I like the Palomar because it doesn't slip on that fluorocarbon line. 

Anyway, I go out to start the truck, and the battery is dead. Just completely dead. Now, I bought that battery at AutoZone not even two years ago. It's a Duralast Gold, supposed to have a three-year replacement warranty. I take it in, and the guy behind the counter—kid couldn't have been more than nineteen, still had acne—he tests it and says there's a parasitic draw. Something was left on. But I know I didn't leave anything on. He did it. He snuck in and drained it just to keep me from going fishing. 

After all these months, I can see it plain as day. He's trying to take over my place. He wants to push me out of my own house, ruin my weekends, mess with my gear. And nobody believes me because they haven't caught him in the act. But I know what's happening.

They changed the gloves on us last year, too, and those don't grip the wrenches right... \n\nAnyway, what was the question? Oh, right. No, no voices or ghosts or any of that movie stuff. Just the things actually happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a doctor that you have any medical problems, like issues with your blood sugar, thyroid, or perhaps any history of head injuries?\n\nPatient: Medical problems? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, my blood sugar is fine, thyroid is fine. I've always been pretty healthy. Honestly, the only time I ever really had to go to the doctor was back when my son was playing Little League. I think it was '98 or '99? He was playing shortstop, and I was helping coach the team. We had this one kid, Tommy, who threw wild. I mean, the boy had an arm like a cannon, but he couldn't aim to save his life. One practice, I wasn't looking—I was trying to untangle the equipment bag because somebody just threw all the catcher's gear in there without strapping it together—and BAM. Baseball right to the shoulder. Hurt like you wouldn't believe, but it wasn't my head. \n\nI did have to go to the clinic for that shoulder, though. They gave me this sling that chafed my neck so bad I couldn't wear it at the plant. You can't be on the assembly line with one arm strapped to your chest anyway, so I just worked through it. You know how it is, if you take time off, they just give your station to one of the new guys, and then you come back and your area is a mess. It takes weeks to get everything organized again. But yeah, no head injuries or diabetes or anything like that. Just that one time with the baseball.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy levels, personality, or your ability to focus and remember things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Energy? No, I got plenty of energy. I work a full shift on my feet, don't I? And my memory is sharp as a tack. I mean, I remember exactly where I leave my tools, like I told you. I can remember things from thirty years ago clear as a bell. Like the old breakroom at the plant before they remodeled it back in '06. \n\nWe used to have this old vending machine in the corner that would always eat your quarters if you tried to buy the peanut butter crackers. You had to hip-check it right on the side—right next to the coin slot—to get your food to drop. Old man Miller, he used to get so mad at that machine. He'd be kicking it, swearing up a storm. He retired, gosh, must be fifteen years ago now. Moved down to Florida. Tampa, I think? Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf side. \n\nThe fishing down there is supposed to be incredible, you know. Redfish, snook... I always wanted to get down there and try saltwater fishing. You need completely different gear for that, though. Heavy-duty reels. The salt just eats right through standard bearings if you don't rinse them off with fresh water the second you get off the boat. I use a little WD-40 on my freshwater reels at home, keeps them spinning nice and smooth, assuming nobody's been in the garage messing with my tackle box. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know what you mean about my personality changing. I'm the exact same guy I've always been. I just want to be left alone in my own house, you know? I don't see how wanting my things left where I put them means I can't focus.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else, perhaps a friend or a neighbor, ever actually seen this relative in your house when you weren't there?\n\nPatient: Seen him? Well, no, because he's sneaky about it. He waits until I'm at the plant. But I'm telling you, I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it. My wife, my neighbors... they look at me like I'm the one who's lost it. But I know my wife's relative is coming into this house to get at me. \n\nMy tools don't move themselves, doc. And it's not just the tools anymore. My fishing plans get spoiled by accident every single weekend. Like last Saturday, I had the truck all packed. I was going to head up to Lake Shelbyville. You ever been up there? It's a man-made reservoir, Army Corps of Engineers built the dam back in the 70s. Great crappie fishing if you know where the submerged timber is. You gotta use these little tube jigs, chartreuse or maybe white with a pink jig head, and just drop it right down into the brush piles. If you're off by a foot, you're snagged. I must have lost a dozen jigs the last time I went. You spend half the morning just tying new knots. The Palomar knot is the best for that, though some guys swear by the improved clinch. I like the Palomar because it doesn't slip on that fluorocarbon line. \n\nAnyway, I go out to start the truck, and the battery is dead. Just completely dead. Now, I bought that battery at AutoZone not even two years ago. It's a Duralast Gold, supposed to have a three-year replacement warranty. I take it in, and the guy behind the counter—kid couldn't have been more than nineteen, still had acne—he tests it and says there's a parasitic draw. Something was left on. But I know I didn't leave anything on. He did it. He snuck in and drained it just to keep me from going fishing. \n\nAfter all these months, I can see it plain as day. He's trying to take over my place. He wants to push me out of my own house, ruin my weekends, mess with my gear. And nobody believes me because they haven't caught him in the act. But I know what's happening.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
At work? No, the guys at the plant are fine. I mean, we all just want to punch in, do our shift, and go home. Most of us have been on the same line for years. You really get to know a guy when you're standing ten feet away from him for eight hours a day. Like Jimmy, the guy at the station next to mine. He's a good guy, but he's got this habit of chewing on toothpicks all day long. Says it helped him quit smoking. He used to smoke two packs of Pall Malls a day. The unfiltered ones, too. I don't know how anybody can smoke those, they just tear your throat up. 

My dad used to smoke unfiltered Chesterfields. He'd sit on the back porch every evening, just puffing away, listening to the Cardinals game on this little transistor radio. He had this old radio, encased in leather, real heavy. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Everything now is just cheap plastic from overseas. You drop it once, and it shatters into a million pieces. I try to buy American-made whenever I can, especially tools. You buy a good set of Craftsman wrenches—the older ones, before they sold the brand—and they'll last you a lifetime. Assuming some relative doesn't come into your garage and start moving them around, that is. 

But no, nobody at the plant is messing with me. They wouldn't care enough to try. It's just him. He's the only one trying to drive me out of my own home.

Somewhere on the Gulf side. \n\nThe fishing down there is supposed to be incredible, you know. Redfish, snook... I always wanted to get down there and try saltwater fishing. You need completely different gear for that, though. Heavy-duty reels. The salt just eats right through standard bearings if you don't rinse them off with fresh water the second you get off the boat. I use a little WD-40 on my freshwater reels at home, keeps them spinning nice and smooth, assuming nobody's been in the garage messing with my tackle box. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know what you mean about my personality changing. I'm the exact same guy I've always been. I just want to be left alone in my own house, you know? I don't see how wanting my things left where I put them means I can't focus.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else, perhaps a friend or a neighbor, ever actually seen this relative in your house when you weren't there?\n\nPatient: Seen him? Well, no, because he's sneaky about it. He waits until I'm at the plant. But I'm telling you, I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it. My wife, my neighbors... they look at me like I'm the one who's lost it. But I know my wife's relative is coming into this house to get at me. \n\nMy tools don't move themselves, doc. And it's not just the tools anymore. My fishing plans get spoiled by accident every single weekend. Like last Saturday, I had the truck all packed. I was going to head up to Lake Shelbyville. You ever been up there? It's a man-made reservoir, Army Corps of Engineers built the dam back in the 70s. Great crappie fishing if you know where the submerged timber is. You gotta use these little tube jigs, chartreuse or maybe white with a pink jig head, and just drop it right down into the brush piles. If you're off by a foot, you're snagged. I must have lost a dozen jigs the last time I went. You spend half the morning just tying new knots. The Palomar knot is the best for that, though some guys swear by the improved clinch. I like the Palomar because it doesn't slip on that fluorocarbon line. \n\nAnyway, I go out to start the truck, and the battery is dead. Just completely dead. Now, I bought that battery at AutoZone not even two years ago. It's a Duralast Gold, supposed to have a three-year replacement warranty. I take it in, and the guy behind the counter—kid couldn't have been more than nineteen, still had acne—he tests it and says there's a parasitic draw. Something was left on. But I know I didn't leave anything on. He did it. He snuck in and drained it just to keep me from going fishing. \n\nAfter all these months, I can see it plain as day. He's trying to take over my place. He wants to push me out of my own house, ruin my weekends, mess with my gear. And nobody believes me because they haven't caught him in the act. But I know what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like anyone else, perhaps people at your work or elsewhere, are also trying to sabotage you or interfere with your life?\n\nPatient: At work? No, the guys at the plant are fine. I mean, we all just want to punch in, do our shift, and go home. Most of us have been on the same line for years. You really get to know a guy when you're standing ten feet away from him for eight hours a day. Like Jimmy, the guy at the station next to mine. He's a good guy, but he's got this habit of chewing on toothpicks all day long. Says it helped him quit smoking. He used to smoke two packs of Pall Malls a day. The unfiltered ones, too. I don't know how anybody can smoke those, they just tear your throat up. \n\nMy dad used to smoke unfiltered Chesterfields. He'd sit on the back porch every evening, just puffing away, listening to the Cardinals game on this little transistor radio. He had this old radio, encased in leather, real heavy. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Everything now is just cheap plastic from overseas. You drop it once, and it shatters into a million pieces. I try to buy American-made whenever I can, especially tools. You buy a good set of Craftsman wrenches—the older ones, before they sold the brand—and they'll last you a lifetime. Assuming some relative doesn't come into your garage and start moving them around, that is. \n\nBut no, nobody at the plant is messing with me. They wouldn't care enough to try. It's just him. He's the only one trying to drive me out of my own home.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

He's the only one trying to drive me out of my own home.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from this relative and the incidents involving your tools and truck, have you ever felt like there were other secret plots or groups of people trying to monitor you or control your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / assembler_or_fabricator / high_school\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, no secret plots or anything crazy like that. I don't know why everyone thinks I'm making up some big conspiracy. It's just him. I mean, who has the time to put together a whole group to monitor somebody? People are too busy these days. Everybody's always rushing around, staring at their phones. You go to the grocery store now, and half the people are looking down at a screen while they push their carts. They just drift right into the middle of the aisle, blocking the whole way. 

I was at the Schnucks just last Tuesday, trying to get down the cereal aisle, and this lady had her cart parked sideways while she was tapping away on her phone. Didn't even look up when I said excuse me. Had to squeeze past her by the oatmeal. I usually buy the store brand oats, anyway. Quaker's fine, but you're just paying for the guy in the hat on the box, you know? The store brand is the exact same rolled oats, probably comes from the same factory in Iowa or wherever. I like to make a big batch on Sunday mornings, put a little brown sugar and some walnuts in it. Walnuts are getting expensive, though. Used to be you could get a big bag for a few bucks, but now they want an arm and a leg. 

But yeah, nobody's monitoring me. I'm just a regular guy trying to eat my oatmeal in peace, if my wife's relative would just stay out of my garage.

It's nice to meet you; what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. Smith, I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm just imagining things, but I know for a fact my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. It’s not in my head. I mean, my tools don't just move themselves. I’ve been an assembler down at the fabrication plant for thirty years, and when you work on a line that long, you learn exactly where you put your things. You have to, or the whole line backs up and the foreman starts yelling. We had this foreman back in the nineties, guys called him 'Bulldog' because of his neck... anyway, I know where I leave my wrenches. But at home, they keep getting moved around. \n\nAnd it's not just that. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. I usually like to head up to the north side of the reservoir—there's a quiet spot right past the old bridge where the water gets real deep, perfect for bass if you use a topwater lure early in the morning. I used to go out there in my old aluminum boat. But lately? Every weekend, something gets messed up on purpose so I can't go. \n\nAfter all these months of this going on, I can see it plain as day. He's been trying to take over my place and push me out of my own home. But my wife and everyone else just look at me like I'm making it all up.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the tools being moved, have you been hearing voices or seeing things that other people don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, doc, no. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. I'm not crazy. My hearing is fine—well, maybe a little ringing sometimes from the stamping presses at the plant, but that's just normal wear and tear from thirty years on the floor. \n\nAnd my eyes are perfectly fine. Although, I will say, it's getting a lot harder to see at work these days because of those new safety glasses they make us wear. You're trying to line up a bracket, and suddenly you're looking through a cloud. I told Dave, our safety rep, I said, 'Dave, how am I supposed to do precision work if I can't even see my own hands?' Dave just shrugs. He’s a decent guy, Dave is, but he’s got his hands tied by corporate up in Chicago. Those guys in suits, they’ve never worked a day on the line in their lives, but they want to tell us how to see. They changed the gloves on us last year, too, and those don't grip the wrenches right... \n\nAnyway, what was the question? Oh, right. No, no voices or ghosts or any of that movie stuff. Just the things actually happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a doctor that you have any medical problems, like issues with your blood sugar, thyroid, or perhaps any history of head injuries?\n\nPatient: Medical problems? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, my blood sugar is fine, thyroid is fine. I've always been pretty healthy. Honestly, the only time I ever really had to go to the doctor was back when my son was playing Little League. I think it was '98 or '99? He was playing shortstop, and I was helping coach the team. We had this one kid, Tommy, who threw wild. I mean, the boy had an arm like a cannon, but he couldn't aim to save his life. One practice, I wasn't looking—I was trying to untangle the equipment bag because somebody just threw all the catcher's gear in there without strapping it together—and BAM. Baseball right to the shoulder. Hurt like you wouldn't believe, but it wasn't my head. \n\nI did have to go to the clinic for that shoulder, though. They gave me this sling that chafed my neck so bad I couldn't wear it at the plant. You can't be on the assembly line with one arm strapped to your chest anyway, so I just worked through it. You know how it is, if you take time off, they just give your station to one of the new guys, and then you come back and your area is a mess. It takes weeks to get everything organized again. But yeah, no head injuries or diabetes or anything like that. Just that one time with the baseball.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy levels, personality, or your ability to focus and remember things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Energy? No, I got plenty of energy. I work a full shift on my feet, don't I? And my memory is sharp as a tack. I mean, I remember exactly where I leave my tools, like I told you. I can remember things from thirty years ago clear as a bell. Like the old breakroom at the plant before they remodeled it back in '06. \n\nWe used to have this old vending machine in the corner that would always eat your quarters if you tried to buy the peanut butter crackers. You had to hip-check it right on the side—right next to the coin slot—to get your food to drop. Old man Miller, he used to get so mad at that machine. He'd be kicking it, swearing up a storm. He retired, gosh, must be fifteen years ago now. Moved down to Florida. Tampa, I think? Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf side. \n\nThe fishing down there is supposed to be incredible, you know. Redfish, snook... I always wanted to get down there and try saltwater fishing. You need completely different gear for that, though. Heavy-duty reels. The salt just eats right through standard bearings if you don't rinse them off with fresh water the second you get off the boat. I use a little WD-40 on my freshwater reels at home, keeps them spinning nice and smooth, assuming nobody's been in the garage messing with my tackle box. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know what you mean about my personality changing. I'm the exact same guy I've always been. I just want to be left alone in my own house, you know? I don't see how wanting my things left where I put them means I can't focus.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else, perhaps a friend or a neighbor, ever actually seen this relative in your house when you weren't there?\n\nPatient: Seen him? Well, no, because he's sneaky about it. He waits until I'm at the plant. But I'm telling you, I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it. My wife, my neighbors... they look at me like I'm the one who's lost it. But I know my wife's relative is coming into this house to get at me. \n\nMy tools don't move themselves, doc. And it's not just the tools anymore. My fishing plans get spoiled by accident every single weekend. Like last Saturday, I had the truck all packed. I was going to head up to Lake Shelbyville. I must have lost a dozen jigs the last time I went. You spend half the morning just tying new knots. The Palomar knot is the best for that, though some guys swear by the improved clinch. I like the Palomar because it doesn't slip on that fluorocarbon line. \n\nAnyway, I go out to start the truck, and the battery is dead. Just completely dead. ---------- turn 8 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gemini-3.1-pro-preview ----------
========== INPUT [patient] ==========
[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a patient who has come to a psychiatric clinic with Delusional Disorder. You are about\nto talk with the doctor.\n\nLooking at the dialogue so far and the doctor's last question, work out what it is asking\nabout, and pick the item of your symptom profile to answer it with.\n\n[Symptom Profiles]\n- key: S019\n  name: Delusions\n  description: Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\n  manifestation: I’m here because everybody keeps acting like I’m imagining it, but I know my wife’s relative came into this house to get at me—my tools don’t move themselves, my fishing plans don’t get spoiled by accident every weekend, and after all these months I can see plain as day he’s been trying to take over my place and push me out.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Functioning is not markedly impaired apart from the impact of the delusion(s) or its ramifications\n  manifestation: I still get up, go to the plant, do my assembler work, keep the bills paid, and take care of myself like I always have. The trouble is all centered on what’s happening in my house with him and my things.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: not required\n  manifestation: I’ve stopped leaving my equipment where I used to, spend a lot of time locking things up and checking the workshop, and I avoid being around him at home. It’s made things tense with my wife, but I’m still managing work and daily responsibilities.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 9 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on for about nine months, ever since my wife’s relative moved into our home.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 54 / assembler_or_fabricator / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S019\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Has anyone else, perhaps a friend or a neighbor, ever actually seen this relative in your house when you weren't there?\n\nPatient: Seen him? Well, no, because he's sneaky about it. He waits until I'm at the plant. But I'm telling you, I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it. My wife, my neighbors... they look at me like I'm the one who's lost it. But I know my wife's relative is coming into this house to get at me. \n\nMy tools don't move themselves, doc. And it's not just the tools anymore. My fishing plans get spoiled by accident every single weekend. Like last Saturday, I had the truck all packed. I was going to head up to Lake Shelbyville. You ever been up there? It's a man-made reservoir, Army Corps of Engineers built the dam back in the 70s. Great crappie fishing if you know where the submerged timber is. You gotta use these little tube jigs, chartreuse or maybe white with a pink jig head, and just drop it right down into the brush piles. If you're off by a foot, you're snagged. I must have lost a dozen jigs the last time I went. You spend half the morning just tying new knots. The Palomar knot is the best for that, though some guys swear by the improved clinch. I like the Palomar because it doesn't slip on that fluorocarbon line. \n\nAnyway, I go out to start the truck, and the battery is dead. Just completely dead. Now, I bought that battery at AutoZone not even two years ago. It's a Duralast Gold, supposed to have a three-year replacement warranty. I take it in, and the guy behind the counter—kid couldn't have been more than nineteen, still had acne—he tests it and says there's a parasitic draw. Something was left on. But I know I didn't leave anything on. He did it. He snuck in and drained it just to keep me from going fishing. \n\nAfter all these months, I can see it plain as day. He's trying to take over my place. He wants to push me out of my own house, ruin my weekends, mess with my gear. And nobody believes me because they haven't caught him in the act. But I know what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like anyone else, perhaps people at your work or elsewhere, are also trying to sabotage you or interfere with your life?\n\nPatient: At work? No, the guys at the plant are fine. I mean, we all just want to punch in, do our shift, and go home. Most of us have been on the same line for years. You really get to know a guy when you're standing ten feet away from him for eight hours a day. Like Jimmy, the guy at the station next to mine. He's a good guy, but he's got this habit of chewing on toothpicks all day long. Says it helped him quit smoking. He used to smoke two packs of Pall Malls a day. The unfiltered ones, too. I don't know how anybody can smoke those, they just tear your throat up. \n\nMy dad used to smoke unfiltered Chesterfields. He'd sit on the back porch every evening, just puffing away, listening to the Cardinals game on this little transistor radio. He had this old radio, encased in leather, real heavy. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Everything now is just cheap plastic from overseas. You drop it once, and it shatters into a million pieces. I try to buy American-made whenever I can, especially tools. You buy a good set of Craftsman wrenches—the older ones, before they sold the brand—and they'll last you a lifetime. Assuming some relative doesn't come into your garage and start moving them around, that is. \n\nBut no, nobody at the plant is messing with me. They wouldn't care enough to try. It's just him. He's the only one trying to drive me out of my own home.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this relative and the incidents involving your tools and truck, have you ever felt like there were other secret plots or groups of people trying to monitor you or control your life?\n\nPatient: No, no secret plots or anything crazy like that. I don't know why everyone thinks I'm making up some big conspiracy. It's just him. I mean, who has the time to put together a whole group to monitor somebody? People are too busy these days. Everybody's always rushing around, staring at their phones. You go to the grocery store now, and half the people are looking down at a screen while they push their carts. They just drift right into the middle of the aisle, blocking the whole way. \n\nI was at the Schnucks just last Tuesday, trying to get down the cereal aisle, and this lady had her cart parked sideways while she was tapping away on her phone. Didn't even look up when I said excuse me. Had to squeeze past her by the oatmeal. I usually buy the store brand oats, anyway. Quaker's fine, but you're just paying for the guy in the hat on the box, you know? The store brand is the exact same rolled oats, probably comes from the same factory in Iowa or wherever. I like to make a big batch on Sunday mornings, put a little brown sugar and some walnuts in it. Walnuts are getting expensive, though. Used to be you could get a big bag for a few bucks, but now they want an arm and a leg. \n\nBut yeah, nobody's monitoring me. I'm just a regular guy trying to eat my oatmeal in peace, if my wife's relative would just stay out of my garage.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever seen a doctor or a counselor about these concerns before, or have you ever been prescribed any medication to help you feel calmer or more at ease?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Doctors? No, I haven't been to any doctors for this. I mean, why would I? I'm not sick, I don't need pills to calm down. I just need him to stop sneaking around my property. The only doctor I usually see is Dr. Evans over at the clinic for my blood pressure, and that's just a checkup once a year. He's a good guy, Dr. Evans. Always wears these really loud ties, like bright yellow with little golf clubs on them. I asked him about it once, and he said his grandkids buy them for him every Father's Day, so he feels like he has to wear them. 

My kids never bought me ties. They usually just get me a gift card to Bass Pro Shops, which is fine by me. You can always use more tackle. Last year I used it to buy a new tackle box, one of those Plano ones with the pull-out trays. The old one I had was metal, rusted out on the bottom because I left it in the bottom of the johnboat after a rainstorm. You really gotta watch out for standing water in those aluminum boats. The rivets start to leak if you aren't careful. Some guys use that flex seal stuff you see on TV, the guy with the screen door in the bottom of the boat? I always wondered if that stuff actually works or if it's just a TV trick. 

Anyway, no, no counselors or medications or anything. I don't need a pill, doc, I just need a lock he can't pick.

I’ve been an assembler down at the fabrication plant for thirty years, and when you work on a line that long, you learn exactly where you put your things. You have to, or the whole line backs up and the foreman starts yelling. We had this foreman back in the nineties, guys called him 'Bulldog' because of his neck... anyway, I know where I leave my wrenches. But at home, they keep getting moved around. \n\nAnd it's not just that. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. I usually like to head up to the north side of the reservoir—there's a quiet spot right past the old bridge where the water gets real deep, perfect for bass if you use a topwater lure early in the morning. I used to go out there in my old aluminum boat. But lately? Every weekend, something gets messed up on purpose so I can't go. \n\nAfter all these months of this going on, I can see it plain as day. He's been trying to take over my place and push me out of my own home. But my wife and everyone else just look at me like I'm making it all up.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the tools being moved, have you been hearing voices or seeing things that other people don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, doc, no. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. I'm not crazy. My hearing is fine—well, maybe a little ringing sometimes from the stamping presses at the plant, but that's just normal wear and tear from thirty years on the floor. \n\nAnd my eyes are perfectly fine. Although, I will say, it's getting a lot harder to see at work these days because of those new safety glasses they make us wear. They brought them in a few years back—said the old ones weren't up to the new OSHA codes or something. But these new ones, they scratch so easy, and they fog up the second you get a sweat going. You're trying to line up a bracket, and suddenly you're looking through a cloud. I told Dave, our safety rep, I said, 'Dave, how am I supposed to do precision work if I can't even see my own hands?' Dave just shrugs. He’s a decent guy, Dave is, but he’s got his hands tied by corporate up in Chicago. Those guys in suits, they’ve never worked a day on the line in their lives, but they want to tell us how to see. They changed the gloves on us last year, too, and those don't grip the wrenches right... \n\nAnyway, what was the question? Oh, right. No, no voices or ghosts or any of that movie stuff. Just the things actually happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a doctor that you have any medical problems, like issues with your blood sugar, thyroid, or perhaps any history of head injuries?\n\nPatient: Medical problems? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, my blood sugar is fine, thyroid is fine. I've always been pretty healthy. Honestly, the only time I ever really had to go to the doctor was back when my son was playing Little League. I think it was '98 or '99? He was playing shortstop, and I was helping coach the team. We had this one kid, Tommy, who threw wild. I mean, the boy had an arm like a cannon, but he couldn't aim to save his life. One practice, I wasn't looking—I was trying to untangle the equipment bag because somebody just threw all the catcher's gear in there without strapping it together—and BAM. Baseball right to the shoulder. Hurt like you wouldn't believe, but it wasn't my head. \n\nI did have to go to the clinic for that shoulder, though. They gave me this sling that chafed my neck so bad I couldn't wear it at the plant. You can't be on the assembly line with one arm strapped to your chest anyway, so I just worked through it. You know how it is, if you take time off, they just give your station to one of the new guys, and then you come back and your area is a mess. It takes weeks to get everything organized again. No, no, nothing like that. I mean, my blood sugar is fine, thyroid is fine. I've always been pretty healthy. We had this one kid, Tommy, who threw wild. I mean, the boy had an arm like a cannon, but he couldn't aim to save his life. One practice, I wasn't looking—I was trying to untangle the equipment bag because somebody just threw all the catcher's gear in there without strapping it together—and BAM. Baseball right to the shoulder. Hurt like you wouldn't believe, but it wasn't my head. \n\nI did have to go to the clinic for that shoulder, though. They gave me this sling that chafed my neck so bad I couldn't wear it at the plant. You can't be on the assembly line with one arm strapped to your chest anyway, so I just worked through it. You know how it is, if you take time off, they just give your station to one of the new guys, and then you come back and your area is a mess. It takes weeks to get everything organized again. But yeah, no head injuries or diabetes or anything like that. Just that one time with the baseball.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy levels, personality, or your ability to focus and remember things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Energy? No, I got plenty of energy. I work a full shift on my feet, don't I? And my memory is sharp as a tack. I mean, I remember exactly where I leave my tools, like I told you. I can remember things from thirty years ago clear as a bell. You had to hip-check it right on the side—right next to the coin slot—to get your food to drop. Old man Miller, he used to get so mad at that machine. He'd be kicking it, swearing up a storm. He retired, gosh, must be fifteen years ago now. Moved down to Florida. Tampa, I think? Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf side. \n\nThe fishing down there is supposed to be incredible, you know. Redfish, snook... I always wanted to get down there and try saltwater fishing. You need completely different gear for that, though. Heavy-duty reels. The salt just eats right through standard bearings if you don't rinse them off with fresh water the second you get off the boat. I use a little WD-40 on my freshwater reels at home, keeps them spinning nice and smooth, assuming nobody's been in the garage messing with my tackle box. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know what you mean about my personality changing. I'm the exact same guy I've always been. I just want to be left alone in my own house, you know? And it's not just the tools anymore. My fishing plans get spoiled by accident every single weekend. Like last Saturday, I had the truck all packed. I was going to head up to Lake Shelbyville. You ever been up there? It's a man-made reservoir, Army Corps of Engineers built the dam back in the 70s. Great crappie fishing if you know where the submerged timber is. You gotta use these little tube jigs, chartreuse or maybe white with a pink jig head, and just drop it right down into the brush piles. If you're off by a foot, you're snagged. I must have lost a dozen jigs the last time I went. You spend half the morning just tying new knots. The Palomar knot is the best for that, though some guys swear by the improved clinch. I like the Palomar because it doesn't slip on that fluorocarbon line. \n\nAnyway, I go out to start the truck, and the battery is dead. Just completely dead. Now, I bought that battery at AutoZone not even two years ago. It's a Duralast Gold, supposed to have a three-year replacement warranty. I take it in, and the guy behind the counter—kid couldn't have been more than nineteen, still had acne—he tests it and says there's a parasitic draw. Something was left on. But I know I didn't leave anything on. He did it. He snuck in and drained it just to keep me from going fishing. \n\nAfter all these months, I can see it plain as day. He's trying to take over my place. He wants to push me out of my own house, ruin my weekends, mess with my gear. And nobody believes me because they haven't caught him in the act. But I know what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like anyone else, perhaps people at your work or elsewhere, are also trying to sabotage you or interfere with your life?\n\nPatient: At work? No, the guys at the plant are fine. I mean, we all just want to punch in, do our shift, and go home. Most of us have been on the same line for years. You really get to know a guy when you're standing ten feet away from him for eight hours a day. Like Jimmy, the guy at the station next to mine. He's a good guy, but he's got this habit of chewing on toothpicks all day long. Says it helped him quit smoking. He used to smoke two packs of Pall Malls a day. The unfiltered ones, too. I don't know how anybody can smoke those, they just tear your throat up. \n\nMy dad used to smoke unfiltered Chesterfields. He'd sit on the back porch every evening, just puffing away, listening to the Cardinals game on this little transistor radio. He had this old radio, encased in leather, real heavy. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Everything now is just cheap plastic from overseas. You drop it once, and it shatters into a million pieces. I try to buy American-made whenever I can, especially tools. You buy a good set of Craftsman wrenches—the older ones, before they sold the brand—and they'll last you a lifetime. Assuming some relative doesn't come into your garage and start moving them around, that is. \n\nBut no, nobody at the plant is messing with me. They wouldn't care enough to try. It's just him. He's the only one trying to drive me out of my own home.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this relative and the incidents involving your tools and truck, have you ever felt like there were other secret plots or groups of people trying to monitor you or control your life?\n\nPatient: No, no secret plots or anything crazy like that. Quaker's fine, but you're just paying for the guy in the hat on the box, you know? The store brand is the exact same rolled oats, probably comes from the same factory in Iowa or wherever. I like to make a big batch on Sunday mornings, put a little brown sugar and some walnuts in it. Walnuts are getting expensive, though. Used to be you could get a big bag for a few bucks, but now they want an arm and a leg. \n\nBut yeah, nobody's monitoring me. I'm just a regular guy trying to eat my oatmeal in peace, if my wife's relative would just stay out of my garage.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen a doctor or a counselor about these concerns before, or have you ever been prescribed any medication to help you feel calmer or more at ease?\n\nPatient: Doctors? No, I haven't been to any doctors for this. I mean, why would I? I'm not sick, I don't need pills to calm down. I just need him to stop sneaking around my property. The only doctor I usually see is Dr. Evans over at the clinic for my blood pressure, and that's just a checkup once a year. He's a good guy, Dr. Evans. Last year I used it to buy a new tackle box, one of those Plano ones with the pull-out trays. The old one I had was metal, rusted out on the bottom because I left it in the bottom of the johnboat after a rainstorm. You really gotta watch out for standing water in those aluminum boats. The rivets start to leak if you aren't careful. Some guys use that flex seal stuff you see on TV, the guy with the screen door in the bottom of the boat? I always wondered if that stuff actually works or if it's just a TV trick. \n\nAnyway, no, no counselors or medications or anything. Well, it's been going on for about nine months now. Started right after he moved into our house. We had that spare room, you know, the one at the back of the hallway that used to be my son's room before he moved out to Phoenix. Phoenix is too hot for me, honestly. I went out there to visit him a couple of years ago in July, and it was like walking into an oven the second you stepped out of the airport. People say, "Oh, it's a dry heat," but let me tell you, when it's a hundred and fifteen degrees, dry or wet, it doesn't matter. You're just roasting. 

My son loves it, though. He's got this little stucco house with a cactus in the front yard. No grass to mow, which I guess is a plus. I spend half my weekends in the summer just trying to keep the lawn looking decent. I've got this old Toro push mower, the kind with the Briggs & Stratton engine. Those engines are bulletproof if you change the oil and keep the spark plug clean. I always drain the gas out of it before winter, too. If you leave that ethanol gas sitting in the carburetor all winter, it gums everything up, and you'll be pulling that cord until your shoulder gives out come spring. 

I actually had to replace the pull cord on it last year. The original one just frayed and snapped right in the middle of mowing the front yard. I had to run up to the hardware store—the local one, not the big box place, because the big box places never have the right diameter cord—and fix it on the driveway. 

But yeah, nine months of him being in the house, nine months of my stuff getting messed with.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF22\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, I'm Dr. Smith. It's nice to meet you; what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, Dr. Smith, I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm just imagining things, but I know for a fact my wife's relative came into this house to get at me. It’s not in my head. I mean, my tools don't just move themselves. I’ve been an assembler down at the fabrication plant for thirty years, and when you work on a line that long, you learn exactly where you put your things. You have to, or the whole line backs up and the foreman starts yelling. We had this foreman back in the nineties, guys called him 'Bulldog' because of his neck... anyway, I know where I leave my wrenches. But at home, they keep getting moved around. \n\nAnd it's not just that. My fishing plans don't get spoiled by accident every single weekend. I usually like to head up to the north side of the reservoir—there's a quiet spot right past the old bridge where the water gets real deep, perfect for bass if you use a topwater lure early in the morning. I used to go out there in my old aluminum boat. But lately? Every weekend, something gets messed up on purpose so I can't go. \n\nAfter all these months of this going on, I can see it plain as day. He's been trying to take over my place and push me out of my own home. But my wife and everyone else just look at me like I'm making it all up.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the tools being moved, have you been hearing voices or seeing things that other people don't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: Voices? No, doc, no. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. I'm not crazy. My hearing is fine—well, maybe a little ringing sometimes from the stamping presses at the plant, but that's just normal wear and tear from thirty years on the floor. \n\nAnd my eyes are perfectly fine. Although, I will say, it's getting a lot harder to see at work these days because of those new safety glasses they make us wear. They brought them in a few years back—said the old ones weren't up to the new OSHA codes or something. But these new ones, they scratch so easy, and they fog up the second you get a sweat going. You're trying to line up a bracket, and suddenly you're looking through a cloud. I told Dave, our safety rep, I said, 'Dave, how am I supposed to do precision work if I can't even see my own hands?' Dave just shrugs. He’s a decent guy, Dave is, but he’s got his hands tied by corporate up in Chicago. Those guys in suits, they’ve never worked a day on the line in their lives, but they want to tell us how to see. They changed the gloves on us last year, too, and those don't grip the wrenches right... \n\nAnyway, what was the question? Oh, right. No, no voices or ghosts or any of that movie stuff. Just the things actually happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a doctor that you have any medical problems, like issues with your blood sugar, thyroid, or perhaps any history of head injuries?\n\nPatient: Medical problems? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, my blood sugar is fine, thyroid is fine. I've always been pretty healthy. Honestly, the only time I ever really had to go to the doctor was back when my son was playing Little League. I think it was '98 or '99? He was playing shortstop, and I was helping coach the team. We had this one kid, Tommy, who threw wild. I mean, the boy had an arm like a cannon, but he couldn't aim to save his life. One practice, I wasn't looking—I was trying to untangle the equipment bag because somebody just threw all the catcher's gear in there without strapping it together—and BAM. Baseball right to the shoulder. Hurt like you wouldn't believe, but it wasn't my head. \n\nI did have to go to the clinic for that shoulder, though. They gave me this sling that chafed my neck so bad I couldn't wear it at the plant. You can't be on the assembly line with one arm strapped to your chest anyway, so I just worked through it. You know how it is, if you take time off, they just give your station to one of the new guys, and then you come back and your area is a mess. It takes weeks to get everything organized again. But yeah, no head injuries or diabetes or anything like that. Just that one time with the baseball.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy levels, personality, or your ability to focus and remember things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Energy? No, I got plenty of energy. I work a full shift on my feet, don't I? And my memory is sharp as a tack. I mean, I remember exactly where I leave my tools, like I told you. I can remember things from thirty years ago clear as a bell. Like the old breakroom at the plant before they remodeled it back in '06. \n\nWe used to have this old vending machine in the corner that would always eat your quarters if you tried to buy the peanut butter crackers. You had to hip-check it right on the side—right next to the coin slot—to get your food to drop. Old man Miller, he used to get so mad at that machine. He'd be kicking it, swearing up a storm. He retired, gosh, must be fifteen years ago now. Moved down to Florida. Tampa, I think? Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf side. \n\nThe fishing down there is supposed to be incredible, you know. Redfish, snook... I always wanted to get down there and try saltwater fishing. You need completely different gear for that, though. Heavy-duty reels. The salt just eats right through standard bearings if you don't rinse them off with fresh water the second you get off the boat. I use a little WD-40 on my freshwater reels at home, keeps them spinning nice and smooth, assuming nobody's been in the garage messing with my tackle box. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know what you mean about my personality changing. I'm the exact same guy I've always been. I just want to be left alone in my own house, you know? I don't see how wanting my things left where I put them means I can't focus.\n\nDoctor: Has anyone else, perhaps a friend or a neighbor, ever actually seen this relative in your house when you weren't there?\n\nPatient: Seen him? Well, no, because he's sneaky about it. He waits until I'm at the plant. But I'm telling you, I'm here because everybody keeps acting like I'm imagining it. My wife, my neighbors... they look at me like I'm the one who's lost it. But I know my wife's relative is coming into this house to get at me. \n\nMy tools don't move themselves, doc. And it's not just the tools anymore. My fishing plans get spoiled by accident every single weekend. Like last Saturday, I had the truck all packed. I was going to head up to Lake Shelbyville. You ever been up there? It's a man-made reservoir, Army Corps of Engineers built the dam back in the 70s. Great crappie fishing if you know where the submerged timber is. You gotta use these little tube jigs, chartreuse or maybe white with a pink jig head, and just drop it right down into the brush piles. If you're off by a foot, you're snagged. I must have lost a dozen jigs the last time I went. You spend half the morning just tying new knots. The Palomar knot is the best for that, though some guys swear by the improved clinch. I like the Palomar because it doesn't slip on that fluorocarbon line. \n\nAnyway, I go out to start the truck, and the battery is dead. Just completely dead. Now, I bought that battery at AutoZone not even two years ago. It's a Duralast Gold, supposed to have a three-year replacement warranty. I take it in, and the guy behind the counter—kid couldn't have been more than nineteen, still had acne—he tests it and says there's a parasitic draw. Something was left on. But I know I didn't leave anything on. He did it. He snuck in and drained it just to keep me from going fishing. \n\nAfter all these months, I can see it plain as day. He's trying to take over my place. He wants to push me out of my own house, ruin my weekends, mess with my gear. And nobody believes me because they haven't caught him in the act. But I know what's happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like anyone else, perhaps people at your work or elsewhere, are also trying to sabotage you or interfere with your life?\n\nPatient: At work? No, the guys at the plant are fine. I mean, we all just want to punch in, do our shift, and go home. Most of us have been on the same line for years. You really get to know a guy when you're standing ten feet away from him for eight hours a day. Like Jimmy, the guy at the station next to mine. He's a good guy, but he's got this habit of chewing on toothpicks all day long. Says it helped him quit smoking. He used to smoke two packs of Pall Malls a day. The unfiltered ones, too. I don't know how anybody can smoke those, they just tear your throat up. \n\nMy dad used to smoke unfiltered Chesterfields. He'd sit on the back porch every evening, just puffing away, listening to the Cardinals game on this little transistor radio. He had this old radio, encased in leather, real heavy. You don't see craftsmanship like that anymore. Everything now is just cheap plastic from overseas. You drop it once, and it shatters into a million pieces. I try to buy American-made whenever I can, especially tools. You buy a good set of Craftsman wrenches—the older ones, before they sold the brand—and they'll last you a lifetime. Assuming some relative doesn't come into your garage and start moving them around, that is. \n\nBut no, nobody at the plant is messing with me. They wouldn't care enough to try. It's just him. He's the only one trying to drive me out of my own home.\n\nDoctor: Aside from this relative and the incidents involving your tools and truck, have you ever felt like there were other secret plots or groups of people trying to monitor you or control your life?\n\nPatient: No, no secret plots or anything crazy like that. I don't know why everyone thinks I'm making up some big conspiracy. It's just him. I mean, who has the time to put together a whole group to monitor somebody? People are too busy these days. Everybody's always rushing around, staring at their phones. You go to the grocery store now, and half the people are looking down at a screen while they push their carts. They just drift right into the middle of the aisle, blocking the whole way. \n\nI was at the Schnucks just last Tuesday, trying to get down the cereal aisle, and this lady had her cart parked sideways while she was tapping away on her phone. Didn't even look up when I said excuse me. Had to squeeze past her by the oatmeal. I usually buy the store brand oats, anyway. Quaker's fine, but you're just paying for the guy in the hat on the box, you know? The store brand is the exact same rolled oats, probably comes from the same factory in Iowa or wherever. I like to make a big batch on Sunday mornings, put a little brown sugar and some walnuts in it. Walnuts are getting expensive, though. Used to be you could get a big bag for a few bucks, but now they want an arm and a leg. \n\nBut yeah, nobody's monitoring me. I'm just a regular guy trying to eat my oatmeal in peace, if my wife's relative would just stay out of my garage.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever seen a doctor or a counselor about these concerns before, or have you ever been prescribed any medication to help you feel calmer or more at ease?\n\nPatient: Doctors? No, I haven't been to any doctors for this. I mean, why would I? I'm not sick, I don't need pills to calm down. I just need him to stop sneaking around my property. The only doctor I usually see is Dr. Evans over at the clinic for my blood pressure, and that's just a checkup once a year. He's a good guy, Dr. Evans. Always wears these really loud ties, like bright yellow with little golf clubs on them. I asked him about it once, and he said his grandkids buy them for him every Father's Day, so he feels like he has to wear them. \n\nMy kids never bought me ties. They usually just get me a gift card to Bass Pro Shops, which is fine by me. You can always use more tackle. Last year I used it to buy a new tackle box, one of those Plano ones with the pull-out trays. The old one I had was metal, rusted out on the bottom because I left it in the bottom of the johnboat after a rainstorm. You really gotta watch out for standing water in those aluminum boats. The rivets start to leak if you aren't careful. Some guys use that flex seal stuff you see on TV, the guy with the screen door in the bottom of the boat? I always wondered if that stuff actually works or if it's just a TV trick. \n\nAnyway, no, no counselors or medications or anything. I don't need a pill, doc, I just need a lock he can't pick.\n\nDoctor: About how long has this been going on with your wife's relative?\n\nPatient: Well, it's been going on for about nine months now. Started right after he moved into our house. We had that spare room, you know, the one at the back of the hallway that used to be my son's room before he moved out to Phoenix. Phoenix is too hot for me, honestly. I went out there to visit him a couple of years ago in July, and it was like walking into an oven the second you stepped out of the airport. The original one just frayed and snapped right in the middle of mowing the front yard. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Jumbled thoughts? No, my mind's fine. I don't get confused or blank out or anything. I'm sharp as a tack. You have to be, working on the assembly line. I've been doing fabrication for, what, almost thirty years now? You lose your focus for one second on the line, and you could lose a finger. Or worse. We had a guy back in '98, Tommy, who wasn't paying attention when the press came down. Messed up his hand real bad. He couldn't work the line after that, had to go on disability. 

I think he moved down to Florida after that. A lot of guys do that, move down to Florida or the Carolinas when they can't work anymore. The taxes are better, I hear. My brother-in-law, not the one living with us, the other one, he bought a timeshare down in Orlando. Tries to get us to go down there every year, but I tell him, I'm not spending my vacation standing in line for two hours just to ride a rollercoaster with a giant mouse. Plus, timeshares are a racket. You pay all this money up front, and then they hit you with maintenance fees every year. It's like a leaky faucet that just keeps dripping money out of your wallet. 

I had a leaky faucet in the guest bathroom a couple months ago. Kept me awake at night, just drip, drip, drip. I finally went in there and took the whole handle apart. Turned out it was just a worn-out O-ring. A ten-cent piece of rubber was causing all that racket. I bought a whole assorted pack of O-rings at the hardware store just so I'd have them on hand. You never know when you're gonna need one. 

But no, my mind doesn't go blank. I know exactly what's going on, especially with him messing with my tools.

He's been trying to take over my place and push me out of my own home. No, doc, no. I don't hear voices or see things that aren't there. I'm not crazy. My hearing is fine—well, maybe a little ringing sometimes from the stamping presses at the plant, but that's just normal wear and tear from thirty years on the floor. \n\nAnd my eyes are perfectly fine. Although, I will say, it's getting a lot harder to see at work these days because of those new safety glasses they make us wear. You're trying to line up a bracket, and suddenly you're looking through a cloud. I told Dave, our safety rep, I said, 'Dave, how am I supposed to do precision work if I can't even see my own hands?' Dave just shrugs. He’s a decent guy, Dave is, but he’s got his hands tied by corporate up in Chicago. Those guys in suits, they’ve never worked a day on the line in their lives, but they want to tell us how to see. Just the things actually happening right in front of me.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been told by a doctor that you have any medical problems, like issues with your blood sugar, thyroid, or perhaps any history of head injuries?\n\nPatient: Medical problems? No, no, nothing like that. I mean, my blood sugar is fine, thyroid is fine. I've always been pretty healthy. Honestly, the only time I ever really had to go to the doctor was back when my son was playing Little League. I think it was '98 or '99? He was playing shortstop, and I was helping coach the team. We had this one kid, Tommy, who threw wild. I mean, the boy had an arm like a cannon, but he couldn't aim to save his life. One practice, I wasn't looking—I was trying to untangle the equipment bag because somebody just threw all the catcher's gear in there without strapping it together—and BAM. Baseball right to the shoulder. Hurt like you wouldn't believe, but it wasn't my head. \n\nI did have to go to the clinic for that shoulder, though. They gave me this sling that chafed my neck so bad I couldn't wear it at the plant. You can't be on the assembly line with one arm strapped to your chest anyway, so I just worked through it. You know how it is, if you take time off, they just give your station to one of the new guys, and then you come back and your area is a mess. It takes weeks to get everything organized again. But yeah, no head injuries or diabetes or anything like that. Just that one time with the baseball.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your energy levels, personality, or your ability to focus and remember things over the past few years?\n\nPatient: Energy? No, I got plenty of energy. I work a full shift on my feet, don't I? And my memory is sharp as a tack. I mean, I remember exactly where I leave my tools, like I told you. You had to hip-check it right on the side—right next to the coin slot—to get your food to drop. Old man Miller, he used to get so mad at that machine. He'd be kicking it, swearing up a storm. He retired, gosh, must be fifteen years ago now. Moved down to Florida. Tampa, I think? Or maybe Sarasota. Somewhere on the Gulf side. \n\nThe fishing down there is supposed to be incredible, you know. Redfish, snook... I always wanted to get down there and try saltwater fishing. You need completely different gear for that, though. Heavy-duty reels. The salt just eats right through standard bearings if you don't rinse them off with fresh water the second you get off the boat. I use a little WD-40 on my freshwater reels at home, keeps them spinning nice and smooth, assuming nobody's been in the garage messing with my tackle box. \n\nBut yeah, I don't know what you mean about my personality changing. I'm the exact same guy I've always been. I just want to be left alone in my own house, you know?
